I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a curry with:
- Initial roux: R0 grams
- Initial water: W0 liters
- Current concentration: R0/W0 g/L

We want to achieve a target concentration C g/L by adding:
- X roux (each of R grams) - X must be a non-negative integer
- Y water (in liters) - Y can be a non-negative real number

The final concentration is: (R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

We need to find the minimum value of X (number of roux to add).

## Mathematical Analysis

From the concentration formula:
(R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

Solving for Y:
R0 + X×R = C × (W0 + Y)
R0 + X×R = C×W0 + C×Y
C×Y = R0 + X×R - C×W0
Y = (R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C

For Y to be valid (non-negative):
(R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C ≥ 0

Since C > 0 (all values are positive integers), we need:
R0 + X×R - C×W0 ≥ 0
X×R ≥ C×W0 - R0
X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

## Key Insights

1. If C ≤ R0/W0 (target concentration ≤ initial concentration), we might not need to add roux. We could just add water to dilute.

2. If C > R0/W0 (target concentration > initial concentration), we need to add roux to increase concentration.

3. We want the minimum X such that Y ≥ 0.

4. X must be an integer ≥ 0.

## Cases to Consider

**Case 1: C ≤ R0/W0**
- We can achieve the target by only adding water (X = 0)
- This works when (R0 + 0×R) / (W0 + Y) = C for some Y ≥ 0
- This gives Y = (R0 - C×W0) / C
- For Y ≥ 0, we need R0 ≥ C×W0
- If R0 ≥ C×W0, then X = 0 works
- If R0 < C×W0, then we need to add roux

**Case 2: C > R0/W0**
- We definitely need to add roux
- We need X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R
- Minimum X is ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)

Wait, let me reconsider the constraint:
We need (R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C
This gives us: C×(W0 + Y) = R0 + X×R
So: C×Y = R0 + X×R - C×W0
For Y ≥ 0: R0 + X×R ≥ C×W0
Therefore: X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

Since X must be an integer, X_min = ceil(max(0, (C×W0 - R0) / R))

But we also need to check if X = 0 is possible:
- X = 0 gives us R0 / (W0 + Y) = C
- This requires Y = (R0/C) - W0
- For Y ≥ 0: R0/C ≥ W0, which means R0 ≥ C×W0

So algorithm:
1. If R0 ≥ C×W0, then X = 0 (we can just add water)
2. Otherwise, X = ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: R0=10, W0=5, C=3, R=4
- C×W0 = 3×5 = 15
- R0 = 10 < 15
- Need X ≥ (15-10)/4 = 5/4 = 1.25
- So X_min = 2 ✓

Example 2: R0=2, W0=5, C=2, R=3  
- C×W0 = 2×5 = 10
- R0 = 2 < 10
- Need X ≥ (10-2)/3 = 8/3 ≈ 2.67
- So X_min = 3 ✓

Example 3: R0=91, W0=13, C=7, R=62
- C×W0 = 7×13 = 91
- R0 = 91 ≥ 91
- So X = 0 ✓

Example 4: R0=10, W0=1, C=3, R=5
- C×W0 = 3×1 = 3
- R0 = 10 ≥ 3
- So X = 0 ✓

Example 5: R0=20, W0=100, C=2, R=20
- C×W0 = 2×100 = 200
- R0 = 20 < 200
- Need X ≥ (200-20)/20 = 180/20 = 9
- So X_min = 9 ✓

Example 6: R0=2, W0=14, C=7, R=1
- C×W0 = 7×14 = 98
- R0 = 2 < 98
- Need X ≥ (98-2)/1 = 96
- So X_min = 96 ✓

Perfect! The solution works.

```python
import math

while True:
    line = input().strip()
    R0, W0, C, R = map(int, line.split())
    
    if R0 == 0 and W0 == 0 and C == 0 and R == 0:
        break
    
    # Check if we can achieve concentration C by adding only water (X = 0)
    # For X = 0: R0 / (W0 + Y) = C, so Y = R0/C - W0
    # For Y >= 0: R0/C >= W0, which means R0 >= C * W0
    if R0 >= C * W0:
        print(0)
    else:
        # We need to add roux
        # We need X >= (C * W0 - R0) / R
        # Since X must be integer, we take ceiling
        needed = (C * W0 - R0) / R
        x_min = math.ceil(needed)
        print(x_min)
```

Curry making

When you use this roux, you think that the curry with a concentration of C [g / L] is the most delicious, so add some roux and water to this curry appropriately to make the concentration C [g / L]. I want to. Here, the concentration of curry in which roux R0 [g] is dissolved in water W0 [L] is R0 / W0 [g / L], and X roux of R [g] and water Y [L] are added to this curry. ] Is added, the concentration becomes (R0 + XR) / (W0 + Y) [g / L]. Although there are a lot of roux, you thought that it was not good to use too much, so you decided to make a curry with a concentration of C [g / L] by reducing the number of roux to be added as much as possible.

When making a curry with a concentration of C [g / L] by properly adding either roux, water, or both to a curry with a concentration of R0 / W0 [g / L], the number of roux X to be added Find the minimum value.

However, please note the following points regarding the curry making this time.

* Number of roux to be added The value of X must be an integer greater than or equal to 0. In other words, it is not possible to add only 1/3 of the roux.
* The value of the volume Y of water to be added can be a real number greater than or equal to 0, and does not have to be an integer.
* In some cases, it is possible to make curry with a concentration of C without adding either roux, water, or both.
* Since a sufficient amount of roux and water is secured, it is good that the situation that the curry with concentration C cannot be made due to lack of roux and water does not occur.



Input

The input consists of multiple datasets. Each dataset consists of one line and is expressed in the following format.

> R0 W0 C R

Here, R0, W0, C, and R are the mass of roux already dissolved in the curry that is being made [g], the volume of water contained in the curry [L], and the concentration of the curry you want to make [g / L]. , Represents the mass [g] per roux. All of these values ​​are integers between 1 and 100. The end of the input is indicated by a line of four zeros separated by blanks.

Output

For each dataset, one line is the minimum number of roux that needs to be added to make a curry with a concentration of C from a fake curry that is a mixture of W0 [L] water and R0 [g] roux. To output. Do not output the amount of water to add.

Note that due to input constraints, the minimum number of roux that is the answer for each dataset is guaranteed to be within the range represented by a 32-bit signed integer.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start with a curry that has R0 grams of roux dissolved in W0 liters of water
2. We want to achieve a target concentration C grams per liter
3. We can add X roux (each of mass R grams) and/or Y liters of water
4. We need to find the minimum integer X (number of roux to add) such that the final concentration is exactly C g/L
5. The final concentration is: (R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

## Mathematical Analysis

From the concentration formula:
(R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

Rearranging:
R0 + X×R = C × (W0 + Y)

R0 + X×R = C×W0 + C×Y

C×Y = R0 + X×R - C×W0

Y = (R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C

For Y to be valid (non-negative real number):
- Y ≥ 0, so (R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C ≥ 0
- Since C > 0, we need R0 + X×R - C×W0 ≥ 0
- Therefore: X×R ≥ C×W0 - R0
- So: X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

But we also need to consider cases where we might not need to add any roux.

1. **Initial concentration already equals target**: If R0/W0 = C, then X = 0
2. **We need to increase concentration**: Current concentration < target
3. **We need to decrease concentration**: Current concentration > target

(R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

This means: R0 + X×R = C×(W0 + Y)

We need Y ≥ 0, so:

X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

So the minimum X is the smallest integer X such that X ≥ max(0, (C×W0 - R0) / R)

- If R0/W0 < C, we need to add roux (since adding water would dilute it)
- If R0/W0 > C, we can either add water (no roux) or add both

This matches the expected output of 3.

## Implementation

```python
import math

while True:
    line = input().strip()
    if line == "0 0 0 0":
        break
    
    R0, W0, C, R = map(int, line.split())
    
    # Calculate initial concentration
    initial_concentration = R0 / W0
    
    # If initial concentration is already at target or higher, we can just add water
    if initial_concentration >= C:
        print(0)
    else:
        # We need to add roux to increase concentration
        # Need: (R0 + X*R) / (W0 + Y) = C
        # So: R0 + X*R = C*(W0 + Y)
        # Y = (R0 + X*R - C*W0) / C >= 0
        # So: R0 + X*R - C*W0 >= 0
        # X*R >= C*W0 - R0
        # X >= (C*W0 - R0) / R
        
        numerator = C * W0 - R0
        if numerator <= 0:
            print(0)
        else:
            # Need X >= numerator / R
            # Minimum integer X is ceil(numerator / R)
            x_min = math.ceil(numerator / R)
            print(x_min)
```

Wait, I can simplify the condition. If initial_concentration >= C, then X=0. If initial_concentration < C, then we need X >= (C*W0 - R0)/R.

But since initial_concentration < C means R0/W0 < C, which means R0 < C*W0, so C*W0 - R0 > 0. So we only need to compute the ceiling when needed.

Actually, I can combine it:

```python
import math

while True:
    line = input().strip()
    if line == "0 0 0 0":
        break
    
    R0, W0, C, R = map(int, line.split())
    
    # We need to add roux only when current concentration < target concentration
    # Current concentration = R0/W0, target = C
    # If R0/W0 >= C, we can achieve target by adding water only
    # If R0/W0 < C, we need to add roux
    
    if R0 >= C * W0:
        print(0)
    else:
        # We need X such that (R0 + X*R) >= C*W0
        # X*R >= C*W0 - R0
        # X >= (C*W0 - R0) / R
        x_min = math.ceil((C * W0 - R0) / R)
        print(x_min)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have:
- Initial curry with R0 grams of roux in W0 liters of water
- Target concentration C grams per liter
- Each roux has mass R grams
- We can add X roux (integer ≥ 0) and Y liters of water (real number ≥ 0)
- We want to minimize X (number of roux to add)

The concentration after adding X roux and Y water is: (R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

We need to find the minimum X such that we can achieve concentration C.

## Mathematical Analysis

From the concentration equation:
(R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

Solving for Y:
R0 + X×R = C × (W0 + Y)
R0 + X×R = C×W0 + C×Y
C×Y = R0 + X×R - C×W0
Y = (R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C

For Y to be valid (≥ 0):
(R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C ≥ 0

Since C > 0 (given constraints), we need:
R0 + X×R - C×W0 ≥ 0
X×R ≥ C×W0 - R0
X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

## Case Analysis

1. **If initial concentration ≥ target concentration**: 
   - We can just add water (Y ≥ 0) without adding any roux
   - X = 0
   - This happens when R0/W0 ≥ C

2. **If initial concentration < target concentration**:
   - We need to add roux to increase concentration
   - We need X ≥ ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)
   - But we also need to check if the resulting Y is non-negative

Actually, let me be more precise:

If (R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C, then:
- We can always achieve the target by choosing Y appropriately
- We only need X such that (R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C for some Y ≥ 0
- Since Y can be any non-negative real, the constraint is just that (R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C is solvable
- This is solvable as long as (R0 + X×R) ≥ C×(W0 + Y) for some Y ≥ 0
- Actually, we can solve for Y: Y = (R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C
- For Y ≥ 0: R0 + X×R - C×W0 ≥ 0
- So X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

But if C×W0 - R0 ≤ 0, then X = 0 works (we just add water).

So the minimum X is:
- 0 if R0/W0 ≥ C (initial concentration ≥ target)
- ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R) if R0/W0 < C (initial concentration < target)

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: R0=10, W0=5, C=3, R=4
- Initial concentration: 10/5 = 2 g/L
- Target: 3 g/L
- Since 2 < 3, we need to add roux
- Need X ≥ (3×5 - 10)/4 = (15-10)/4 = 5/4 = 1.25
- So minimum X = 2 (ceiling of 1.25)

This matches the expected output of 2.

Example 2: R0=2, W0=5, C=2, R=3
- Initial concentration: 2/5 = 0.4 g/L
- Target: 2 g/L
- Since 0.4 < 2, we need to add roux
- Need X ≥ (2×5 - 2)/3 = (10-2)/3 = 8/3 ≈ 2.67
- So minimum X = 3 (ceiling of 2.67)

This matches the expected output of 3.

Example 3: R0=91, W0=13, C=7, R=62
- Initial concentration: 91/13 ≈ 7 g/L
- Target: 7 g/L
- Since 91/13 = 7, initial concentration = target
- So X = 0

This matches the expected output of 0.

## Algorithm

For each dataset:
1. Read R0, W0, C, R
2. If R0/W0 ≥ C, output 0
3. Otherwise, calculate X = ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)
4. Output X

```python
import math

while True:
    line = input().strip()
    if line == "0 0 0 0":
        break
    
    R0, W0, C, R = map(int, line.split())
    
    # Calculate initial concentration
    initial_concentration = R0 / W0
    
    # If initial concentration is already at or above target
    if initial_concentration >= C:
        print(0)
    else:
        # Need to add roux
        # We need: (R0 + X*R) / (W0 + Y) = C
        # Solving for Y: Y = (R0 + X*R - C*W0) / C
        # For Y >= 0: R0 + X*R - C*W0 >= 0
        # So X >= (C*W0 - R0) / R
        
        numerator = C * W0 - R0
        # Since initial_concentration < C, numerator > 0
        x_min = (numerator + R - 1) // R  # This is ceiling division
        print(x_min)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Curry making

As the ACM-ICPC domestic qualifying is approaching, you who wanted to put more effort into practice decided to participate in a competitive programming camp held at a friend's house. Participants decided to prepare their own meals.

On the first night of the training camp, the participants finished the day's practice and began preparing dinner. Not only in competitive programming, but also in self-catering, you are often said to be a "professional", and you have spared time because you have finished making the menu for your charge in a blink of an eye. Therefore, I decided to help other people make curry.

Now, there is a curry made by mixing R0 [g] roux with W0 [L] water. There is one type of roux used this time, and each roux is R [g]. Ru has a sufficient stockpile. When you use this roux, you think that the curry with a concentration of C [g / L] is the most delicious, so add some roux and water to this curry appropriately to make the concentration C [g / L]. I want to. Here, the concentration of curry in which roux R0 [g] is dissolved in water W0 [L] is R0 / W0 [g / L], and X roux of R [g] and water Y [L] are added to this curry. ] Is added, the concentration becomes (R0 + XR) / (W0 + Y) [g / L]. Although there are a lot of roux, you thought that it was not good to use too much, so you decided to make a curry with a concentration of C [g / L] by reducing the number of roux to be added as much as possible.

When making a curry with a concentration of C [g / L] by properly adding either roux, water, or both to a curry with a concentration of R0 / W0 [g / L], the number of roux X to be added Find the minimum value.

However, please note the following points regarding the curry making this time.

* Number of roux to be added The value of X must be an integer greater than or equal to 0. In other words, it is not possible to add only 1/3 of the roux.
* The value of the volume Y of water to be added can be a real number greater than or equal to 0, and does not have to be an integer.
* In some cases, it is possible to make curry with a concentration of C without adding either roux, water, or both.
* Since a sufficient amount of roux and water is secured, it is good that the situation that the curry with concentration C cannot be made due to lack of roux and water does not occur.



Input

The input consists of multiple datasets. Each dataset consists of one line and is expressed in the following format.

> R0 W0 C R

Here, R0, W0, C, and R are the mass of roux already dissolved in the curry that is being made [g], the volume of water contained in the curry [L], and the concentration of the curry you want to make [g / L]. , Represents the mass [g] per roux. All of these values ​​are integers between 1 and 100. The end of the input is indicated by a line of four zeros separated by blanks.

Output

For each dataset, one line is the minimum number of roux that needs to be added to make a curry with a concentration of C from a fake curry that is a mixture of W0 [L] water and R0 [g] roux. To output. Do not output the amount of water to add.

Note that due to input constraints, the minimum number of roux that is the answer for each dataset is guaranteed to be within the range represented by a 32-bit signed integer.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a curry with:
- Initial roux: R0 grams
- Initial water: W0 liters
- Initial concentration: R0/W0 g/L

We want to achieve a target concentration C g/L by adding:
- X roux (each of R grams) - X must be an integer ≥ 0
- Y water (in liters) - Y can be any real number ≥ 0

The final concentration is: (R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

We need to find the minimum value of X (number of roux to add).

## Mathematical Analysis

From the concentration equation:
(R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

Solving for Y:
R0 + X×R = C × (W0 + Y)
R0 + X×R = C×W0 + C×Y
C×Y = R0 + X×R - C×W0
Y = (R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C

For Y to be valid (≥ 0), we need:
(R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C ≥ 0

Since C > 0 (all inputs are positive), this means:
R0 + X×R - C×W0 ≥ 0
X×R ≥ C×W0 - R0
X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

## Special Cases

1. If initial concentration R0/W0 ≥ C: We can achieve target concentration by only adding water (X = 0)
2. If initial concentration R0/W0 < C: We need to add roux to increase concentration

## Algorithm

1. Check if X = 0 is valid (i.e., can we achieve concentration C by only adding water?)
   - This happens when R0/W0 ≥ C (initial concentration ≥ target concentration)
   - When R0/W0 ≥ C, by adding water, we dilute the mixture, so concentration decreases
   - So if R0/W0 ≥ C, we cannot achieve concentration C by just adding water
   - Actually, if R0/W0 ≥ C, we can only get concentrations ≥ C by adding roux or not adding anything
   - Wait, let me reconsider...

Actually, if R0/W0 ≥ C, then adding water will only decrease the concentration, so we cannot achieve a concentration C < R0/W0 by adding water. 

But if R0/W0 < C, we need to add roux to increase the concentration.

So:
- If R0/W0 ≥ C: We can achieve C by adding water (X = 0)
- If R0/W0 < C: We need to add roux

For R0/W0 < C, we need X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

Since X must be an integer ≥ 0, the minimum X is:
X = max(0, ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R))

Wait, let me check this again.

Actually, from the equation:
Y = (R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C ≥ 0

So we need R0 + X×R - C×W0 ≥ 0
X×R ≥ C×W0 - R0
X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

If (C×W0 - R0) ≤ 0, then X ≥ 0, so minimum X = 0
If (C×W0 - R0) > 0, then X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R, so minimum X = ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)

But we also need to check if we can achieve concentration C without adding roux (X = 0):
- For X = 0, we have concentration = R0/W0
- We want R0/W0 = C
- So if R0/W0 = C, then X = 0 is valid
- If R0/W0 > C, then we cannot achieve C by adding water (it would only make it worse)
- If R0/W0 < C, we need to add roux

Actually, if R0/W0 > C, we cannot achieve C by just adding water (adding water only decreases concentration). But we might be able to achieve C by adding roux.

Let me reconsider the equation:
(R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

For any X ≥ 0, we can always find Y ≥ 0 to satisfy this equation, as long as the numerator is positive and we can solve for Y.

The constraint is that Y ≥ 0, so:
Y = (R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C ≥ 0
R0 + X×R ≥ C×W0
X×R ≥ C×W0 - R0
X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

So the minimum X is:
- If (C×W0 - R0) ≤ 0, then X ≥ 0, so minimum X = 0  
- If (C×W0 - R0) > 0, then X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R, so minimum X = ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)

However, if initially R0/W0 ≥ C, then (C×W0 - R0) ≤ 0, so X = 0 works.
If initially R0/W0 < C, then (C×W0 - R0) > 0, so we need X ≥ positive value.

But if R0/W0 ≥ C, and we try X = 0, we get concentration R0/W0 ≥ C, which is already ≥ C. We want exactly C.

Actually, since we're adding water when X = 0:
Concentration = R0/W0
We want this to equal C.

So X = 0 works if and only if R0/W0 = C.

If R0/W0 > C, then adding water will decrease concentration further, so we cannot achieve C < R0/W0.

Wait, if R0/W0 > C, we want to achieve C which is smaller. Adding water decreases concentration, so we can achieve any concentration less than R0/W0 by adding water. So we can achieve C if R0/W0 > C.

For example: R0=10, W0=5, C=3, R=4
Initial concentration = 10/5 = 2 g/L
Target = 3 g/L > initial concentration

We need to increase concentration by adding roux.
(R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C
(10 + 4X) / (5 + Y) = 3
10 + 4X = 15 + 3Y
4X - 3Y = 5
We can solve for Y: Y = (4X - 5)/3

For Y ≥ 0: (4X - 5)/3 ≥ 0 → 4X ≥ 5 → X ≥ 5/4 = 1.25 → X ≥ 2 (since X integer)

So minimum X = 2.

The condition for X = 0 to work is R0/W0 = C.
The condition for X > 0 to be needed is when we need to increase concentration (R0/W0 < C) or when we need to decrease concentration (R0/W0 > C)?

Actually, if R0/W0 > C, we want to decrease concentration, which we can do by adding water (X = 0).
If R0/W0 < C, we want to increase concentration, which we can only do by adding roux.

But the formula still holds: we need X ≥ (C×W0 - R0)/R

- If C×W0 - R0 ≤ 0, then X ≥ 0, so minimum X = 0
- If C×W0 - R0 > 0, then X ≥ (C×W0 - R0)/R, so minimum X = ceil((C×W0 - R0)/R)

Let me verify with the first example:
R0=10, W0=5, C=3, R=4
C×W0 - R0 = 3×5 - 10 = 15 - 10 = 5 > 0
X ≥ 5/4 = 1.25 → X ≥ 2 → answer = 2 ✓

Second example: R0=2, W0=5, C=2, R=3
C×W0 - R0 = 2×5 - 2 = 10 - 2 = 8 > 0
X ≥ 8/3 ≈ 2.67 → X ≥ 3 → answer = 3 ✓

Third example: R0=91, W0=13, C=7, R=62
C×W0 - R0 = 7×13 - 91 = 91 - 91 = 0
X ≥ 0 → X = 0 ✓

Fourth example: R0=10, W0=1, C=3, R=5
C×W0 - R0 = 3×1 - 10 = 3 - 10 = -7 < 0
X ≥ 0 → X = 0 ✓

Fifth example: R0=20, W0=100, C=2, R=20
C×W0 - R0 = 2×100 - 20 = 200 - 20 = 180 > 0
X ≥ 180/20 = 9 → X = 9 ✓

Sixth example: R0=2, W0=14, C=7, R=1
C×W0 - R0 = 7×14 - 2 = 98 - 2 = 96 > 0
X ≥ 96/1 = 96 → X = 96 ✓

Perfect! The solution is:
- If C×W0 - R0 ≤ 0, answer is 0
- If C×W0 - R0 > 0, answer is ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)

In code, we can use `(C*W0 - R0 + R - 1) // R` to compute ceiling division.

```python
import sys

def solve():
    while True:
        line = input().strip()
        if line == "0 0 0 0":
            break
            
        R0, W0, C, R = map(int, line.split())
        
        # Calculate the required minimum X
        numerator = C * W0 - R0
        
        if numerator <= 0:
            print(0)
        else:
            # X >= numerator / R, so minimum X is ceil(numerator / R)
            # Ceiling of a/b = (a + b - 1) // b
            x = (numerator + R - 1) // R
            print(x)

solve()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have a curry with:
- Initial roux: R0 grams
- Initial water: W0 liters
- Current concentration: R0/W0 g/L

We want to achieve a target concentration C g/L by adding:
- X roux (each of R grams) - X must be an integer ≥ 0
- Y water (in liters) - Y can be any non-negative real number

The final concentration will be: (R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

We need to find the minimum value of X (number of roux to add).

## Mathematical Analysis

From the concentration formula:
(R0 + X×R) / (W0 + Y) = C

Solving for Y:
R0 + X×R = C × (W0 + Y)
R0 + X×R = C×W0 + C×Y
C×Y = R0 + X×R - C×W0
Y = (R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C

For Y to be valid (non-negative):
(R0 + X×R - C×W0) / C ≥ 0
R0 + X×R - C×W0 ≥ 0
X×R ≥ C×W0 - R0
X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

## Cases to Consider

1. **If current concentration is already C**: X = 0 works (add no roux, no water)
2. **If current concentration is less than C**: We need to add roux (X > 0)
3. **If current concentration is greater than C**: We need to add water to dilute

For case 3 (current concentration > C): We can achieve concentration C by adding water only, so X = 0.

For case 2 (current concentration < C): We need to add roux.

The minimum X must satisfy:
X ≥ max(0, (C×W0 - R0) / R)

Since X must be an integer, we need X ≥ ceil(max(0, (C×W0 - R0) / R))

## Special Case: When (C×W0 - R0) ≤ 0

If C×W0 ≤ R0, then we don't need to add any roux (X = 0), because even with current roux, we have enough concentration.

## Algorithm

1. If current concentration is already ≥ C, answer is 0
2. If current concentration < C, we need to add roux
3. Calculate the minimum X: X = ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)
4. But we also need to ensure the resulting Y ≥ 0

Actually, let me reconsider: the condition is that Y ≥ 0, which means (R0 + X×R - C×W0) ≥ 0.

So we need: X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R

If C×W0 - R0 ≤ 0, then X ≥ 0, so minimum X = 0.

If C×W0 - R0 > 0, then X ≥ (C×W0 - R0) / R, so minimum X = ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)

But wait - we also need to check if X=0 is sufficient. If current concentration is already C, or if we can achieve C by just adding water, then X=0.

The current concentration is R0/W0. If R0/W0 ≥ C, we can achieve C by adding water (or not adding anything).

If R0/W0 < C, we need to add roux.

So:
- If R0/W0 ≥ C: answer is 0
- If R0/W0 < C: answer is ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)

Let me verify with the first example:
R0=10, W0=5, C=3, R=4
Current concentration: 10/5 = 2 g/L
Target: 3 g/L, so we need to increase concentration
R0/W0 = 2 < 3, so we need roux
(C×W0 - R0)/R = (3×5 - 10)/4 = (15-10)/4 = 5/4 = 1.25
So X = ceil(1.25) = 2

This matches the expected output of 2.

Second example:
R0=2, W0=5, C=2, R=3
Current concentration: 2/5 = 0.4 g/L
Target: 2 g/L, so we need to increase concentration
(C×W0 - R0)/R = (2×5 - 2)/3 = (10-2)/3 = 8/3 ≈ 2.67
X = ceil(2.67) = 3

This matches the expected output of 3.

So the algorithm is:
- If R0/W0 ≥ C: return 0
- Else: return ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R)

But we need to be careful about integer division and ceiling.

To compute ceiling of a/b for positive integers a,b:
ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b

However, (C×W0 - R0) might be negative, so we need to be careful.

Actually, if C×W0 - R0 ≤ 0, then (C×W0 - R0) / R ≤ 0, so ceil((C×W0 - R0) / R) ≤ 0, which means X = 0.

So we can write:
if (C * W0) <= R0:
    return 0
else:
    return ((C * W0 - R0) + R - 1) // R

Let me check this with the third example:
R0=91, W0=13, C=7, R=62
Current concentration: 91/13 ≈ 7 g/L, which equals C, so should be 0
C×W0 = 7×13 = 91, which equals R0, so condition is satisfied, return 0. Correct.

Fourth example:
R0=10, W0=1, C=3, R=5
Current concentration: 10/1 = 10 g/L > 3 g/L, so we can achieve target by adding water, return 0. Correct.

Fifth example:
R0=20, W0=100, C=2, R=20
Current concentration: 20/100 = 0.2 g/L < 2 g/L, so need roux
(C×W0 - R0)/R = (2×100 - 20)/20 = (200-20)/20 = 180/20 = 9
ceil(9) = 9. Correct.

Sixth example:
R0=2, W0=14, C=7, R=1
Current concentration: 2/14 ≈ 0.14 < 7, so need roux
(C×W0 - R0)/R = (7×14 - 2)/1 = (98-2)/1 = 96
ceil(96) = 96. Correct.

Perfect! The solution works.

```python
import sys

for line in sys.stdin:
    line = line.strip()
    if line == "0 0 0 0":
        break
    
    R0, W0, C, R = map(int, line.split())
    
    # If current concentration is already >= target concentration, we can achieve target by adding water
    # Current concentration is R0/W0, target is C
    # If R0/W0 >= C, then R0 >= C * W0
    if R0 >= C * W0:
        print(0)
    else:
        # We need to add roux
        # We need (R0 + X*R) / (W0 + Y) = C
        # Solving for Y: Y = (R0 + X*R - C*W0) / C
        # For Y >= 0: (R0 + X*R - C*W0) >= 0
        # So X >= (C*W0 - R0) / R
        # Minimum integer X = ceil((C*W0 - R0) / R)
        numerator = C * W0 - R0
        # Ceiling of numerator/R: (numerator + R - 1) // R
        x = (numerator + R - 1) // R
        print(x)
```

import sys

for line in sys.stdin:
    line = line.strip()
    if line == "0 0 0 0":
        break
    
    R0, W0, C, R = map(int, line.split())
    
    # If current concentration is already >= target concentration, we can achieve target by adding water
    # Current concentration is R0/W0, target is C
    # If R0/W0 >= C, then R0 >= C * W0
    if R0 >= C * W0:
        print(0)
    else:
        # We need to add roux
        # We need (R0 + X*R) / (W0 + Y) = C
        # Solving for Y: Y = (R0 + X*R - C*W0) / C
        # For Y >= 0: (R0 + X*R - C*W0) >= 0
        # So X >= (C*W0 - R0) / R
        # Minimum integer X = ceil((C*W0 - R0) / R)
        numerator = C * W0 - R0
        # Ceiling of numerator/R: (numerator + R - 1) // R
        x = (numerator + R - 1) // R
        print(x)

Results

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have an arithmetic sequence where:
- First element is `a`
- Common difference is `c`
- We need to check if a favorite number `b` appears in this sequence

The sequence looks like: a, a+c, a+2c, a+3c, ...

## Mathematical Analysis

For `b` to be in the sequence, there must exist a positive integer `i` such that:
`a + (i-1) * c = b`

Solving for `i`:
`(i-1) * c = b - a`
`i - 1 = (b - a) / c`
`i = (b - a) / c + 1`

For `i` to be a valid positive integer:
1. `(b - a) / c` must be a non-negative integer (if c > 0)
2. If c = 0, then the sequence is constant: all elements are `a`. So `b` appears only if `b = a`
3. If c ≠ 0, then `(b - a)` must be divisible by `c`, and the result must be non-negative

## Case Analysis

**Case 1: c = 0**
- Sequence is: a, a, a, ...
- `b` appears if and only if `b = a`

**Case 2: c ≠ 0**
- We need `(b - a)` to be divisible by `c`
- And `(b - a) / c ≥ 0` (so that i ≥ 1)

Wait, let me reconsider the condition for a positive integer i:
- We need `(i-1) * c = b - a`
- So `(b - a)` must be divisible by `c`
- And `(b - a) / c ≥ 0` (since i-1 ≥ 0)

Actually, let me think about this more carefully:
- If `c > 0`, then the sequence increases: a, a+c, a+2c, ...
- So `b` appears if `b ≥ a` and `(b - a)` is divisible by `c`
- If `c < 0`, then the sequence decreases: a, a+c, a+2c, ... 
- So `b` appears if `b ≤ a` and `(a - b)` is divisible by `|c|` (same as `(b - a)` divisible by `c`)

Actually, in both cases, for `b` to be in the sequence, we need:
1. `(b - a)` is divisible by `c`
2. If `c > 0`, then `b ≥ a`
3. If `c < 0`, then `b ≤ a`

But notice that if `c < 0`, then `(b - a)` being divisible by `c` implies `(a - b)` is divisible by `|c|`.

However, we can simplify: `(b - a)` must be divisible by `c`, and the quotient `(b - a) / c` must be ≥ 0.

Wait, that's not right. If `c < 0`, and `(b - a) / c ≥ 0`, then `(b - a)` must be ≤ 0.

So the condition is: `(b - a)` is divisible by `c`, and `(b - a) / c ≥ 0`.

But there's a special case when `c = 0`.

## Final Algorithm

```python
if c == 0:
    return b == a
else:
    # Check if (b - a) is divisible by c
    # and (b - a) / c >= 0
    diff = b - a
    if diff % c == 0:
        quotient = diff // c
        return quotient >= 0
    else:
        return False
```

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: a=1, b=7, c=3
- diff = 7-1 = 6
- 6 % 3 == 0 ✓
- quotient = 6 // 3 = 2 ≥ 0 ✓
- Answer: YES ✓

Example 2: a=10, b=10, c=0
- c == 0, so check b == a → 10 == 10 ✓
- Answer: YES ✓

Example 3: a=1, b=-4, c=5
- diff = -4 - 1 = -5
- -5 % 5 == 0 ✓ (since -5 = 5 * (-1))
- quotient = -5 // 5 = -1 < 0 ✗
- Answer: NO ✓

Example 4: a=0, b=60, c=50
- diff = 60 - 0 = 60
- 60 % 50 = 10 ≠ 0 ✗
- Answer: NO ✓

This looks correct!

But wait - in the modulo operation with negative numbers, Python's modulo behaves differently. Let me check: `-5 % 5` should be 0 in Python, which is correct.

The condition `(b - a) % c == 0` will work correctly in Python because it gives the remainder when dividing by c.

However, I need to be careful about the negative division. In Python, `-5 // 5 = -1`, which is correct, and we want quotient ≥ 0.

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())

if c == 0:
    print("YES" if a == b else "NO")
else:
    diff = b - a
    if diff % c == 0:
        quotient = diff // c
        if quotient >= 0:
            print("YES")
        else:
            print("NO")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

Let me verify example 3 again: a=1, b=-4, c=5
- diff = -5
- -5 % 5 == 0 ✓
- quotient = -5 // 5 = -1 < 0 ✗
- Answer: NO ✓

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have an arithmetic sequence where:
- First term: s₁ = a
- Common difference: c (so sᵢ - sᵢ₋₁ = c)
- We need to check if a favorite number b appears in this sequence

The sequence looks like: a, a+c, a+2c, a+3c, ...

## Mathematical Analysis

For b to be in the sequence, there must exist a positive integer i such that:
sᵢ = a + (i-1) × c = b

Solving for i:
a + (i-1) × c = b
(i-1) × c = b - a
i - 1 = (b - a) / c
i = (b - a) / c + 1

For i to be a positive integer:
1. If c = 0: the sequence is constant (all elements equal a). So b must equal a.
2. If c ≠ 0: (b - a) must be divisible by c, and the result must give a positive integer i.

## Special Cases

1. **When c = 0**: The sequence is a, a, a, a, ... 
   - If b = a: YES (b appears)
   - If b ≠ a: NO (b doesn't appear)

2. **When c ≠ 0**: We need (b - a) to be divisible by c, and the resulting index i to be positive.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read a, b, c from input
2. If c = 0:
   - If a == b: return "YES"
   - Else: return "NO"
3. If c ≠ 0:
   - Calculate difference = b - a
   - Check if difference is divisible by c (i.e., difference % c == 0)
   - If divisible, calculate i = (b - a) / c + 1
   - Check if i > 0 (since i must be a positive integer)
   - If both conditions met, return "YES", else "NO"

Wait, I need to be more careful about the positive integer requirement.

Actually, since i = (b - a) / c + 1, and i must be a positive integer:
- (b - a) must be divisible by c
- (b - a) / c must be ≥ 0 (so that i ≥ 1)

## Final Algorithm

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())

if c == 0:
    if a == b:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
else:
    # Check if (b - a) is divisible by c
    diff = b - a
    if diff % c == 0:
        # The index i = (b - a) / c + 1
        # We need i to be positive, so (b - a) / c >= 0
        if diff >= 0:
            print("YES")
        else:
            print("NO")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

Let me verify with the examples:

1. a=1, b=7, c=3: diff = 6, 6 % 3 == 0, diff >= 0 → YES ✓
2. a=10, b=10, c=0: c=0, a=b → YES ✓  
3. a=1, b=-4, c=5: diff = -5, -5 % 5 == 0, but diff = -5 < 0 → NO ✓
4. a=0, b=60, c=50: diff = 60, 60 % 50 = 10 ≠ 0 → NO ✓

Perfect!

```python
    if a == b:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
else:
```

   - Otherwise, `b` is not in the sequence (NO)

2. **When c ≠ 0**: We need `(b - a)` to be divisible by `c`, and the result `(b - a) / c` must be a positive integer.

2. Handle the special case when c = 0
3. When c ≠ 0, check if (b - a) is divisible by c and if the quotient is positive
4. Output "YES" or "NO" accordingly

- If b ≠ a: NO

- We need (b - a) to be divisible by c 
- And (b - a) / c > 0 (positive)

## Problem Understanding

We have an arithmetic sequence where:
- First element is `a` (s₁ = a)
- Common difference is `c` (sᵢ - sᵢ₋₁ = c)
- We need to check if a favorite number `b` appears in this sequence

The sequence looks like: a, a+c, a+2c, a+3c, ...

## Mathematical Analysis

For `b` to appear in the sequence, there must exist a positive integer `i` such that:
- sᵢ = b
- sᵢ = a + (i-1) × c = b

Solving for i:
- a + (i-1) × c = b
- (i-1) × c = b - a
- i - 1 = (b - a) / c
- i = (b - a) / c + 1

For `i` to be a valid positive integer:
1. If c = 0: The sequence is constant (all elements equal to a). So b appears only if b = a.
2. If c ≠ 0: We need (b - a) / c to be a non-negative integer (since i-1 ≥ 0)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Special case: c = 0**
   - If c = 0, all elements are a
   - So b appears if and only if b = a

2. **Case: c ≠ 0**
   - We need (b - a) to be divisible by c
   - And (b - a) / c ≥ 0 (so that i ≥ 1)
   - This means (b - a) ≥ 0 when c > 0
   - This means (b - a) ≤ 0 when c < 0 (because dividing by negative reverses inequality)

3. **Check conditions:**
   - If c = 0: check if b == a
   - If c ≠ 0: check if (b - a) is divisible by c and (b - a) / c ≥ 0

## Implementation

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())

if c == 0:
    # Sequence is constant: all elements equal to a
    if a == b:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
else:
    # c ≠ 0
    # We need (b - a) to be divisible by c and (b - a) / c >= 0
    diff = b - a
    
    # Check if diff is divisible by c
    if diff % c == 0:
        # Check if quotient is non-negative
        quotient = diff // c
        if quotient >= 0:
            print("YES")
        else:
            print("NO")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

## Verification with Examples

1. **Input: 1 7 3**
   - a=1, b=7, c=3
   - diff = 7-1 = 6
   - 6 % 3 == 0 ✓
   - quotient = 6 // 3 = 2 ≥ 0 ✓
   - Output: YES ✓

2. **Input: 10 10 0**
   - c = 0
   - a = 10, b = 10
   - a == b ✓
   - Output: YES ✓

3. **Input: 1 -4 5**
   - a=1, b=-4, c=5

   - -5 % 5 == 0 ✓
   - quotient = -5 // 5 = -1 < 0 ✗
   - Output: NO ✓

4. **Input: 0 60 50**
   - a=0, b=60, c=50
   - diff = 60-0 = 60
   - 60 % 50 = 10 ≠ 0 ✗

    # We need (b - a) to be divisible by c and (b - a) / c >= 0
    diff = b - a
    
    # Check if diff is divisible by c
    if diff % c == 0:
        # Check if quotient is non-negative
        quotient = diff // c
        if quotient >= 0:
            print("YES")
        else:
            print("NO")
    else:
        print("NO")

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have an arithmetic sequence where:
- First element s₁ = a
- Common difference between consecutive elements = c
- We need to check if a favorite number b appears in this sequence

The sequence looks like: a, a+c, a+2c, a+3c, ...

## Mathematical Analysis

For b to appear in the sequence, there must exist a positive integer i such that:
sᵢ = a + (i-1) × c = b

Solving for i:
a + (i-1) × c = b
(i-1) × c = b - a
i - 1 = (b - a) / c
i = (b - a) / c + 1

For i to be a positive integer:
1. If c = 0: the sequence is constant (all elements are a). So b appears only if b = a.
2. If c ≠ 0: (b - a) must be divisible by c, and the resulting i must be a positive integer (i > 0).

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Handle special case when c = 0**:
   - If c = 0, then all elements are equal to a
   - b appears only if b = a

2. **Handle case when c ≠ 0**:
   - Check if (b - a) is divisible by c
   - If divisible, calculate i = (b - a) / c + 1
   - Check if i > 0 (positive integer)

3. **Important consideration**: We need to ensure that (b - a) is divisible by c, meaning (b - a) % c == 0

4. **Edge cases**:
   - When c = 0, we already handled it
   - When c ≠ 0, we need to check divisibility

## Implementation

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())

if c == 0:
    # Sequence is constant: all elements are 'a'
    if a == b:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
else:
    # Check if (b - a) is divisible by c
    diff = b - a
    if diff % c == 0:
        # Calculate i: i = (b - a) / c + 1
        i = diff // c + 1
        # Check if i is positive (i > 0)
        if i > 0:
            print("YES")
        else:
            print("NO")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

## Verification with examples:

1. **Input: 1 7 3**
   - a=1, b=7, c=3
   - diff = 7-1 = 6
   - 6 % 3 = 0 ✓
   - i = 6//3 + 1 = 2 + 1 = 3 > 0 ✓
   - Output: YES ✓

2. **Input: 10 10 0**
   - c = 0
   - a = b = 10
   - Output: YES ✓

3. **Input: 1 -4 5**
   - a=1, b=-4, c=5
   - diff = -4-1 = -5
   - -5 % 5 = 0 ✓
   - i = -5//5 + 1 = -1 + 1 = 0
   - i = 0 is not > 0, so output: NO ✓

4. **Input: 0 60 50**
   - a=0, b=60, c=50
   - diff = 60-0 = 60
   - 60 % 50 = 10 ≠ 0
   - Output: NO ✓

Wait, let me reconsider example 3. The difference is -5, and -5 % 5 = 0 in Python (since -5 = (-1) × 5 + 0), so it should be divisible. But we get i = 0, which is not positive.

So the condition is correct: we need i > 0.

Actually, in the calculation:
- diff = b - a = -4 - 1 = -5
- i = (-5) // 5 + 1 = -1 + 1 = 0
- Since i must be a positive integer (i > 0), we output NO.

This matches the expected output.

The solution is correct.

```python

    if a == b:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have an arithmetic sequence where:
- First element s₁ = a
- Common difference = c
- So the sequence is: a, a+c, a+2c, a+3c, ...

We need to determine if a given number b appears in this sequence.

## Mathematical Analysis

For b to appear in the sequence, there must exist a positive integer i such that:
sᵢ = a + (i-1) × c = b

Solving for i:
a + (i-1) × c = b
(i-1) × c = b - a
i - 1 = (b - a) / c
i = (b - a) / c + 1

For i to be a valid positive integer:
1. (b - a) / c must be a non-negative integer (since i > 0)
2. If c = 0, then the sequence is constant: a, a, a, ...
   - In this case, b appears only if b = a

## Case Analysis

**Case 1: c = 0**
- Sequence is constant: a, a, a, ...
- b appears if and only if b = a

**Case 2: c ≠ 0**
- We need (b - a) to be divisible by c
- And (b - a) / c ≥ 0 (so that i ≥ 1)
- This means: (b - a) must be non-negative and divisible by c

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read inputs a, b, c
2. If c = 0:
   - If b == a, output "YES"
   - Otherwise, output "NO"
3. If c ≠ 0:
   - Check if (b - a) is divisible by c
   - Check if (b - a) ≥ 0
   - If both conditions are true, output "YES"
   - Otherwise, output "NO"

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1:** a=1, b=7, c=3
- c ≠ 0
- (b-a) = 6
- 6 % 3 = 0 (divisible)
- 6 ≥ 0 (non-negative)
- Output: "YES" ✓

**Example 2:** a=10, b=10, c=0
- c = 0
- b = a (10 = 10)
- Output: "YES" ✓

**Example 3:** a=1, b=-4, c=5
- c ≠ 0
- (b-a) = -5
- -5 % 5 = 0 (divisible)
- -5 < 0 (negative)
- Output: "NO" ✓

**Example 4:** a=0, b=60, c=50
- c ≠ 0
- (b-a) = 60
- 60 % 50 = 10 (not divisible)
- Output: "NO" ✓

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())

if c == 0:
    if a == b:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
else:
    diff = b - a
    # Check if diff is divisible by c and non-negative
    if diff >= 0 and diff % c == 0:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have an arithmetic sequence where:
- First element s₁ = a
- Difference between consecutive elements = c
- So the sequence is: a, a+c, a+2c, a+3c, ...

We need to determine if a favorite number b appears in this sequence.

## Mathematical Analysis

For b to be in the sequence, there must exist a positive integer i such that:
sᵢ = a + (i-1) × c = b

Rearranging:
a + (i-1) × c = b
(i-1) × c = b - a
i - 1 = (b - a) / c
i = (b - a) / c + 1

For i to be a positive integer:
1. If c = 0: The sequence is constant (all elements equal a). So b appears if and only if b = a.
2. If c ≠ 0: We need (b - a) / c to be a non-negative integer (since i ≥ 1, so (b - a)/c ≥ 0).

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Case 1: c = 0**
   - Sequence is just a, a, a, a, ...
   - b appears if and only if b = a

2. **Case 2: c ≠ 0**
   - We need (b - a) to be divisible by c
   - And (b - a) / c ≥ 0 (so that i ≥ 1)

Let me verify with examples:
- Example 1: a=1, b=7, c=3
  - (7-1)/3 = 6/3 = 2, which is a non-negative integer
  - So YES ✓

- Example 2: a=10, b=10, c=0
  - c=0, b=a, so YES ✓

- Example 3: a=1, b=-4, c=5
  - (b-a)/c = (-4-1)/5 = -5/5 = -1, which is negative
  - So NO ✓

- Example 4: a=0, b=60, c=50
  - (b-a)/c = (60-0)/50 = 60/50 = 6/5 = 1.2, not an integer
  - So NO ✓

## Implementation

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())

if c == 0:
    # Sequence is constant: a, a, a, ...
    if a == b:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
else:
    # c != 0
    diff = b - a
    # Check if diff is divisible by c and the result is non-negative
    if diff % c == 0 and diff // c >= 0:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

Wait, I need to be careful with the divisibility check. When we say `diff % c == 0`, this works for positive and negative values of c.

But actually, we need `(b-a)/c` to be a non-negative integer. So:
- `(b-a)` must be divisible by `c`
- `(b-a)/c` must be ≥ 0

The code above handles this correctly. Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: (7-1)=6, 6%3=0 and 6//3=2 ≥ 0 → YES ✓
Example 2: c=0, a=10, b=10 → a==b → YES ✓  
Example 3: (b-a)=(-4-1)=-5, -5%5=0 but -5//5=-1 < 0 → NO ✓
Example 4: (b-a)=60, 60%50=10 ≠ 0 → NO ✓

Actually, let me double-check the modulo operation with negative numbers in Python. In Python, the modulo operation always returns a result with the same sign as the divisor. Since we're using positive c, `diff % c == 0` will correctly detect divisibility.

So the solution is correct.

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())

if c == 0:
    if a == b:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
else:
    diff = b - a
    if diff % c == 0 and diff // c >= 0:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have an arithmetic sequence where:
- First element: s₁ = a
- Common difference: c (so sᵢ - sᵢ₋₁ = c)
- We need to check if a specific number b appears in this sequence

The sequence looks like: a, a+c, a+2c, a+3c, ...

## Mathematical Analysis

For b to be in the sequence, there must exist a positive integer i such that:
sᵢ = a + (i-1) × c = b

Solving for i:
a + (i-1) × c = b
(i-1) × c = b - a
i - 1 = (b - a) / c
i = (b - a) / c + 1

For i to be a valid positive integer:
1. (b - a) / c must be a non-negative integer (since i > 0)
2. If c = 0, then the sequence is constant: a, a, a, a, ...
   - In this case, b appears only if b = a

## Special Cases to Handle

1. **When c = 0**: The sequence is constant. So b appears iff b = a.
2. **When c ≠ 0**: We need (b - a) to be divisible by c, and (b - a) / c ≥ 0 (since i-1 ≥ 0).

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read a, b, c from input
2. Handle the special case where c = 0
3. For c ≠ 0, check if (b - a) is divisible by c and if the quotient is non-negative
4. Output "YES" if b is in the sequence, "NO" otherwise

## Implementation

```python
a, b, c = map(int, input().split())

# Special case: if common difference is 0
if c == 0:
    if a == b:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
else:
    # Check if (b - a) is divisible by c and the result is non-negative
    diff = b - a
    if diff % c == 0:
        # Check if the quotient is non-negative (i-1 >= 0)
        quotient = diff // c
        if quotient >= 0:
            print("YES")
        else:
            print("NO")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

1. Input: 1 7 3
   - diff = 7 - 1 = 6
   - 6 % 3 = 0, quotient = 6 // 3 = 2 ≥ 0 → YES ✓

2. Input: 10 10 0
   - c = 0, a = b = 10 → YES ✓

3. Input: 1 -4 5
   - diff = -4 - 1 = -5
   - -5 % 5 = 0, quotient = -5 // 5 = -1 < 0 → NO ✓

4. Input: 0 60 50
   - diff = 60 - 0 = 60
   - 60 % 50 = 10 ≠ 0 → NO ✓

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In this kata, you'll be given an integer of range `0 <= x <= 99` and have to return that number spelt out in English. A few examples:

```python
name_that_number(4)   # returns "four"
name_that_number(19)  # returns "nineteen"
name_that_number(99)  # returns "ninety nine"
```

Words should be separated by only spaces and not hyphens. No need to validate parameters, they will always be in the range [0, 99]. Make sure that the returned String has no leading of trailing spaces. Good luck!

```python
def name_that_number(x):
	
```

Your solution should implemented in the function "name_that_number". The inputs will be passed to it and it should return the correct solution.

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to convert an integer from 0 to 99 into its English word representation
2. Numbers 0-19 have unique names (zero, one, two, ..., nineteen)
3. Numbers 20-99 follow a pattern: tens place + space + units place (if units > 0)
4. No hyphens, just spaces between words
5. No leading or trailing spaces in the output

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify the patterns
- 0-19: have unique names
- 20-99: tens place (twenty, thirty, etc.) + space + units place (if units > 0)

### Step 2: Break down the logic
1. Create arrays for numbers 0-19 and for tens place (20, 30, 40, etc.)
2. Handle special cases for 0-19
3. For 20-99, extract tens and units
4. Combine with proper spacing

### Step 3: Implementation approach
- Create a dictionary or array for words 0-19
- Create a dictionary or array for tens place (20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80, 90)
- For numbers 0-19: return the corresponding word
- For numbers 20-99: get tens word + space + units word (if units > 0)

### Step 4: Handle edge cases
- 0 returns "zero"
- 10 returns "ten" (no units)
- 25 returns "twenty five" (not "twenty-five")

```python
def name_that_number(x):
    # Words for numbers 0-19
    ones = ["zero", "one", "two", "three", "four", "five", "six", "seven", "eight", "nine", "ten", 
            "eleven", "twelve", "thirteen", "fourteen", "fifteen", "sixteen", "seventeen", "eighteen", "nineteen"]
    
    # Words for tens place
    tens = ["", "", "twenty", "thirty", "forty", "fifty", "sixty", "seventy", "eighty", "ninety"]
    
    if x < 20:
        return ones[x]
    else:
        # For numbers 20-99
        tens_digit = x // 10
        ones_digit = x % 10
        
        # If ones digit is 0, just return tens word
        if ones_digit == 0:
            return tens[tens_digit]
        else:
            # Otherwise return tens word + space + ones word
            return tens[tens_digit] + " " + ones[ones_digit]
```

## Problem Understanding

1. Convert a number from 0-99 to its English word representation
2. Handle single digits (0-9), teens (10-19), and tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)
3. Words should be separated by spaces only, no hyphens
4. No leading or trailing spaces in the result

## Step-by-Step Analysis

### Step 1: Break down the number ranges
- 0-9: single digits
- 10-19: teens (special case)
- 20-99: tens (followed by a single digit)

### Step 2: Identify what words we need
- Single digits: zero, one, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight, nine
- Teens: ten, eleven, twelve, thirteen, fourteen, fifteen, sixteen, seventeen, eighteen, nineteen
- Tens: twenty, thirty, forty, fifty, sixty, seventy, eighty, ninety

### Step 3: Algorithm logic
1. Handle 0 as special case
2. For numbers 1-9: use single digit words
3. For numbers 10-19: use teen words
4. For numbers 20-99: combine tens word with single digit (if needed)

### Step 4: Implementation details
- Create dictionaries for single digits and teens
- For tens: 20, 30, etc. - use the tens word
- For numbers like 23: "twenty three" (tens + single digit)
- For numbers like 20: "twenty" (just tens word)

## Implementation

```python
def name_that_number(x):
    # Define words for single digits and teens
    ones = ["zero", "one", "two", "three", "four", "five", "six", "seven", "eight", "nine"]
    teens = ["ten", "eleven", "twelve", "thirteen", "fourteen", "fifteen", "sixteen", "seventeen", "eighteen", "nineteen"]
    tens = ["", "", "twenty", "thirty", "forty", "fifty", "sixty", "seventy", "eighty", "ninety"]
    
    if x == 0:
        return "zero"
    
    if x < 10:
        return ones[x]
    
    if x < 20:
        return teens[x - 10]
    
    # For numbers 20-99
    tens_digit = x // 10
    ones_digit = x % 10
    
    if ones_digit == 0:
        return tens[tens_digit]
    else:
        return tens[tens_digit] + " " + ones[ones_digit]
```

## Verification with examples:

- name_that_number(19) → teens[19-10] → teens[9] → "nineteen" ✓
- name_that_number(99) → tens[9] + " " + ones[9] → "ninety nine" ✓

The solution handles all cases correctly and returns words separated by single spaces with no leading/trailing spaces.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to convert an integer from 0 to 99 into its English word representation
2. Numbers should be spelled out with words separated by single spaces (no hyphens)
3. Examples: 4 → "four", 19 → "nineteen", 99 → "ninety nine"

## Step-by-step Analysis

### Step 1: Break down the number ranges
- 0-19: These have unique names (zero, one, two, ..., nineteen)
- 20-99: These are composed of tens (twenty, thirty, forty, etc.) and units (one, two, etc.)

### Step 2: Identify patterns
- For numbers 20-99: "twenty one" = "twenty one", "thirty four" = "thirty four"
- For numbers 10-19: these are special cases that don't follow the "tens + units" pattern
- Special cases: 10-19 need to be handled separately

### Step 3: Plan the approach
1. Create arrays/strings for units (0-19) and tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)
2. Handle special cases for 0-19
3. For 20-99: combine tens part with units part (with space)
4. Special handling for numbers ending in 0 (like 20, 30)

```python
def name_that_number(x):
    # Units (0-19)
    units = ["zero", "one", "two", "three", "four", "five", "six", "seven", "eight", "nine", 
             "ten", "eleven", "twelve", "thirteen", "fourteen", "fifteen", "sixteen", "seventeen", "eighteen", "nineteen"]
    
    # Tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)
    tens = ["", "", "twenty", "thirty", "forty", "fifty", "sixty", "seventy", "eighty", "ninety"]
    
    if x == 0:
        return "zero"
    
    if x < 20:
        return units[x]
    
    # For numbers 20-99
    ten_place = x // 10
    unit_place = x % 10
    
    if unit_place == 0:
        return tens[ten_place]
    else:
        return tens[ten_place] + " " + units[unit_place]
```

## Problem Understanding

1. Convert a number between 0 and 99 to its English word representation
2. Handle both single digits and two-digit numbers
3. Return words separated by spaces, not hyphens
4. No leading or trailing spaces
5. Examples: 4 → "four", 19 → "nineteen", 99 → "ninety nine"

## Step-by-Step Analysis

### Step 1: Identify patterns
- Numbers 0-19 have unique names
- Numbers 20-99 follow a pattern: (tens place) + (units place, if not zero)
- For 20-99: tens digit + space + units digit (if units > 0)

### Step 2: Break down the solution
- Create a dictionary for 0-19: "zero", "one", "two", ..., "nineteen"
- Create a dictionary for tens: "twenty", "thirty", "forty", "fifty", "sixty", "seventy", "eighty", "ninety"
- Handle special cases:
  - 0: "zero"
  - 1-19: use the special names
  - 20-99: tens name + space + (units name if units > 0)

### Step 3: Algorithm
1. If x is 0, return "zero"
2. If x < 20, return the corresponding word from 0-19
3. If x >= 20:
   - Get the tens digit (x // 10)
   - Get the units digit (x % 10)
   - Return tens_word + " " + (units_word if units > 0 else "")

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to convert an integer from 0 to 99 into its English word representation
2. For numbers 0-19, we have specific names (zero, one, two, ..., nineteen)
3. For numbers 20-99, we break them down into tens and units:
   - 20-29: "twenty", "thirty", etc.
   - 30-39: "thirty", "thirty-one", etc.
4. We need to handle the hyphen case: numbers like 21 should be "twenty one" (not "twenty-one")
5. The output should have no leading/trailing spaces and words should be separated by single spaces

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify the patterns
- Numbers 0-19 have specific names
- Numbers 20-99 have a tens place and possibly a units place
- For numbers 20, 30, 40, etc., we just use the tens word
- For numbers 21, 22, etc., we use "tens word" + "space" + "units word"

### Step 2: Plan the algorithm
1. Create arrays for numbers 0-19 and tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)
2. If x is 0-19, directly return the corresponding word
3. If x is 20-99, get the tens part and units part
4. For 20-99: tens_word + " " + (units_word if units > 0 else "")

### Step 3: Handle edge cases
- x = 0 → "zero"
- x = 19 → "nineteen" (not "ten nine")
- x = 20 → "twenty" (not "twenty zero")

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def name_that_number(x):
    # Words for numbers 0-19
    ones = [
        "zero", "one", "two", "three", "four", "five", 
        "six", "seven", "eight", "nine", "ten", 
        "eleven", "twelve", "thirteen", "fourteen", "fifteen", 
        "sixteen", "seventeen", "eighteen", "nineteen"
    ]
    
    # Words for tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)
    tens = [
        "", "twenty", "thirty", "forty", "fifty", 
        "sixty", "seventy", "eighty", "ninety"
    ]
    
    if x <= 19:
        return ones[x]
    else:
        # For numbers 20-99
        tens_digit = x // 10
        units_digit = x % 10
        
        tens_word = tens[tens_digit]
        units_word = ones[units_digit]
        
        # If units digit is 0, we don't include the units word
        if units_digit == 0:
            return tens_word
        else:
            return tens_word + " " + units_word
```

## Verification with examples:
- name_that_number(4) → ones[4] → "four" ✓
- name_that_number(19) → ones[19] → "nineteen" ✓  
- name_that_number(99) → tens[9] + " " + ones[9] → "ninety nine" ✓

The solution correctly handles all cases within the specified range.

- For numbers 10-19: use special teen mapping
- For numbers 20-99: tens part + " " + units part (if units > 0)

```python
def name_that_number(x):
    # Define word arrays
    ones = ["zero", "one", "two", "three", "four", "five", "six", "seven", "eight", "nine"]
    teens = ["ten", "eleven", "twelve", "thirteen", "fourteen", "fifteen", "sixteen", "seventeen", "eighteen", "nineteen"]
    tens = ["", "", "twenty", "thirty", "forty", "fifty", "sixty", "seventy", "eighty", "ninety"]
    
    if x < 10:
        return ones[x]
    elif x < 20:
        return teens[x - 10]
    else:
        tens_digit = x // 10
        ones_digit = x % 10
        
        # For tens (20, 30, etc.)
        if ones_digit == 0:
            return tens[tens_digit]
        else:
            # For numbers like 21, 34, etc.
            return tens[tens_digit] + " " + ones[ones_digit]
```

## Verification with examples:
- name_that_number(4) → ones[4] → "four" ✓
- name_that_number(19) → teens[9] → "nineteen" ✓  
- name_that_number(99) → tens[9] + " " + ones[9] → "ninety nine" ✓

The solution correctly handles all cases within the given range with proper spacing.

def name_that_number(x):

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Convert a number from 0-99 to its English word representation
2. Handle single digits (0-9)
3. Handle teens (10-19) - special case
4. Handle tens (20, 30, 40, etc.) - "twenty", "thirty", etc.
5. Handle combinations like "ninety nine" for 99
6. No hyphens, just spaces between words
7. No leading or trailing spaces

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **Single digits (0-9)**: Need words "zero", "one", "two", ..., "nine"
2. **Teens (10-19)**: Need special words "ten", "eleven", "twelve", ..., "nineteen"
3. **Other tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)**: Need "twenty", "thirty", "forty", "fifty", "sixty", "seventy", "eighty", "ninety"
4. **Combinations**: For numbers like 23, it should be "twenty three" (not "twenty-three")

## Pattern Recognition

- Numbers 0-9: direct mapping
- Numbers 10-19: special mapping (teens)
- Numbers 20-99: tens place + ones place (if ones > 0)

## Implementation Strategy

1. Create arrays for digit names (0-9)
2. Create array for teens (10-19)
3. Create array for tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)
4. Handle special cases:
   - If x is 0: return "zero"
   - If x is between 10-19: use teens array
   - If x is between 20-99: get tens part + ones part
5. Combine parts with a space

## Edge Cases to Consider

- 0 → "zero"
- 10 → "ten" 
- 11 → "eleven"
- 19 → "nineteen"
- 20 → "twenty"
- 21 → "twenty one"
- 99 → "ninety nine"

## Code Implementation

```python
def name_that_number(x):
    # Single digit words
    ones = ["zero", "one", "two", "three", "four", "five", "six", "seven", "eight", "nine"]
    
    # Teens (10-19)
    teens = ["ten", "eleven", "twelve", "thirteen", "fourteen", "fifteen", "sixteen", "seventeen", "eighteen", "nineteen"]
    
    # Tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)
    tens = ["", "", "twenty", "thirty", "forty", "fifty", "sixty", "seventy", "eighty", "ninety"]
    
    if x == 0:
        return "zero"
    elif x < 10:
        return ones[x]
    elif x < 20:
        return teens[x - 10]
    else:
        # For numbers 20-99
        tens_digit = x // 10
        ones_digit = x % 10
        
        # If ones digit is 0, just return tens part
        if ones_digit == 0:
            return tens[tens_digit]
        else:
            # Otherwise return tens part + space + ones part
            return tens[tens_digit] + " " + ones[ones_digit]
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Convert an integer from 0 to 99 to its English spelling
2. Numbers should be separated by spaces only (not hyphens)
3. No leading or trailing spaces in the output
4. Examples: 4 → "four", 19 → "nineteen", 99 → "ninety nine"

## Step-by-Step Analysis

### Step 1: Break down the number ranges
- 0-19: Have unique names (zero, one, two, ..., nineteen)
- 20-99: Tens place + units place (twenty, thirty, forty, ..., ninety)

### Step 2: Identify patterns
- For numbers 0-19: Direct mapping
- For numbers 20-99: 
  - Tens digit (20, 30, 40, etc.) 
  - Units digit (for 21, 22, etc.)

### Step 3: Handle special cases
- 0: "zero"
- 10-19: Special cases (ten, eleven, twelve, etc.)
- 20, 30, 40, etc.: "twenty", "thirty", etc.
- 21, 22, etc.: "twenty one", "twenty two", etc.

### Step 4: Algorithm design
1. Create arrays for numbers 0-19 and tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)
2. If x is 0-19, return the corresponding word
3. If x is 20-99, get the tens word and units word
4. Combine them with a space

## Implementation

```python
def name_that_number(x):
    # Words for numbers 0-19
    ones = ["zero", "one", "two", "three", "four", "five", "six", "seven", "eight", "nine", 
            "ten", "eleven", "twelve", "thirteen", "fourteen", "fifteen", "sixteen", "seventeen", "eighteen", "nineteen"]
    
    # Words for tens (20, 30, 40, etc.)
    tens = ["", "", "twenty", "thirty", "forty", "fifty", "sixty", "seventy", "eighty", "ninety"]
    
    if x <= 19:
        return ones[x]
    else:
        # For numbers 20-99
        tens_digit = x // 10
        units_digit = x % 10
        
        # Get the tens part (e.g., for 23, tens_digit = 2, so "twenty")
        tens_word = tens[tens_digit]
        
        # Get the units part (e.g., for 23, units_digit = 3, so "three")
        units_word = ones[units_digit] if units_digit > 0 else ""
        
        # Combine with space
        if units_word:
            return tens_word + " " + units_word
        else:
            return tens_word
```

## Verification with examples:
- name_that_number(4) → ones[4] → "four" ✓
- name_that_number(19) → ones[19] → "nineteen" ✓
- name_that_number(99) → tens[9] + " " + ones[9] → "ninety nine" ✓

The solution handles all cases correctly and follows the requirement of no hyphens and proper spacing.
